Using the above proposition, we now obtain the main theorem of this section:

Theorem 3.8

The category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}_1(\mathcal {S}(c,0))$$\end{document}C1(S(c,0)) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1$$\end{document}C1-cofinite grading-restricted generalized \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c,0)$$\end{document}S(c,0)-modules is the same as the category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_c^{\textrm{fin}}$$\end{document}Ocfin of finite-length grading-restricted generalized \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c,0)$$\end{document}S(c,0)-modules whose composition factors are not isomorphic to Verma modules or their parity reversals.

Proof

Proposition 3.7 and Lemma 3.3(3) show that any module in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}_1(\mathcal {S}(c,0))$$\end{document}C1(S(c,0)) has finite length with composition factors of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^n(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h))$$\end{document}Πn(Lns(c,h)) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h \in H_c$$\end{document}h∈Hc and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=0,1$$\end{document}n=0,1. Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}_1(\mathcal {S}(c,0))\subset \mathcal {O}_c^{\textrm{fin}}$$\end{document}C1(S(c,0))⊂Ocfin. Conversely, Lemma 3.2(2) shows that any finite-length module with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1$$\end{document}C1-cofinite composition factors is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1$$\end{document}C1-cofinite, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}^{\textrm{fin}}_c\subset \mathcal {C}_1(\mathcal {S}(c,0))$$\end{document}Ocfin⊂C1(S(c,0)). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Since every simple grading-restricted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c,0)$$\end{document}S(c,0)-module is self-contragredient, and since Theorem 3.8 shows \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}_1(\mathcal {S}(c,0))$$\end{document}C1(S(c,0)) equals the category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}^{\textrm{fin}}_c$$\end{document}Ocfin finite-length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c,0)$$\end{document}S(c,0)-modules whose composition factors are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1$$\end{document}C1-cofinite, Theorem 2.25 implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_c^{\textrm{fin}}$$\end{document}Ocfin is a braided tensor category:

Corollary 3.9

For any central charge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}c∈C, the category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_c^{\textrm{fin}}$$\end{document}Ocfin of finite-length grading-restricted generalized V-modules with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1$$\end{document}C1-cofinite composition factors admits the braided tensor category structure of [47–54].

In the following subsections, we discuss some of the detailed structure of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_c^{\textrm{fin}}$$\end{document}Ocfin for central charges at which semisimple tensor categories occur.

Tensor structure at central charge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^\mathfrak {ns}(t)$$\end{document}cns(t) for irrational t

In this subsection we consider the tensor category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin when3.15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c=c^\mathfrak {ns}(t)=\frac{15}{2}-3(t+t^{-1}), \quad t\in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {Q}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}c=cns(t)=152-3(t+t-1),t∈C\Q.For these central charges, the Verma module embedding diagrams are particularly simple (see Proposition 2.5(3)).

We start by proving that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=c^\mathfrak {ns}(t)$$\end{document}c=cns(t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\notin \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}t∉Q, is semisimple, similar to [21, Theorem 5.1.2]. First note that the simple objects in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin are the irreducible modules \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_{r,s}\,{:}{=}\, L^\mathfrak {ns}(c, h^\mathfrak {ns}_{r,s})$$\end{document}Sr,s:=Lns(c,hr,sns) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s∈Z≥1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r-s\in 2\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}r-s∈2Z, together with their parity reversals. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\notin \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}t∉Q, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^\mathfrak {ns}_{r,s} = h^\mathfrak {ns}_{r',s'}$$\end{document}hr,sns=hr′,s′ns for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s,r',s'\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s,r′,s′∈Z≥1 if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r=r'$$\end{document}r=r′ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=s'$$\end{document}s=s′.

Theorem 3.10

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=c^\mathfrak {ns}(t)$$\end{document}c=cns(t) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\notin \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}t∉Q, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin is semisimple with distinct simple objects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{r,s})$$\end{document}Πn(Sr,s) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=0,1$$\end{document}n=0,1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_+$$\end{document}r,s∈Z+ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r-s\in 2\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}r-s∈2Z.

Proof

Since every module in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin has finite length, it is enough to show that any short exact sequence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0\longrightarrow \Pi ^{n_1}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_1))\longrightarrow M \longrightarrow \Pi ^{n_2}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_2))\longrightarrow 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0⟶Πn1(Lns(c,h1))⟶M⟶Πn2(Lns(c,h2))⟶0such that M is a module in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1,n_2\in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}n1,n2∈Z, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1,h_2\in H_c$$\end{document}h1,h2∈Hc splits. Because operators in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {ns}$$\end{document}ns change conformal weights by half-integers, any such exact sequence splits if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1-h_2\notin \frac{1}{2}\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}h1-h2∉12Z. Moreover, by [39, Lemma 6.2.2], simple modules in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin do not have non-split self-extensions, so the exact sequence splits when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1=h_2$$\end{document}h1=h2.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1-h_2\in \frac{1}{2}\mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}h1-h2∈12Z≥1, then there is a highest-weight vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{h_2}\in M$$\end{document}vh2∈M of conformal weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_2$$\end{document}h2. If N is the submodule of M generated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{h_2}$$\end{document}vh2, then N is a quotient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^{n_2}(M^\mathfrak {ns}(c, h_2))$$\end{document}Πn2(Mns(c,h2)). As t is irrational, the embedding diagram in Proposition 2.5(3) shows that N is isomorphic to either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^{n_2}(M^\mathfrak {ns}(c, h_2))$$\end{document}Πn2(Mns(c,h2)) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^{n_2}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_2))$$\end{document}Πn2(Lns(c,h2)). Since Verma modules are not objects of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin, we get \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N\cong \Pi ^{n_2}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_2))$$\end{document}N≅Πn2(Lns(c,h2)) and the sequence splits.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_2-h_1\in \frac{1}{2}\mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}h2-h1∈12Z≥1, we take contragredient duals to get a short exact sequence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0\longrightarrow \Pi ^{n_2}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_2))\longrightarrow M' \longrightarrow \Pi ^{n_1}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_1))\longrightarrow 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0⟶Πn2(Lns(c,h2))⟶M′⟶Πn1(Lns(c,h1))⟶0.Then by the previous paragraph, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M'\cong \Pi ^{n_1}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_1))\oplus \Pi ^{n_2}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_2))$$\end{document}M′≅Πn1(Lns(c,h1))⊕Πn2(Lns(c,h2)), and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\cong \Pi ^{n_1}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_1))\oplus \Pi ^{n_2}(L^\mathfrak {ns}(c,h_2))$$\end{document}M≅Πn1(Lns(c,h1))⊕Πn2(Lns(c,h2)) as well. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Our next goal is to determine the fusion rules for the simple modules in the semisimple tensor category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=c^\mathfrak {ns}(t)$$\end{document}c=cns(t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\notin \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}t∉Q. Actually, these fusion rules were already determined in [28, Example 7.11] for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi $$\end{document}Π-supercategory version of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin (where we allow odd as well as even morphisms), except that it was not established in [28] that the results apply to every central charge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^\mathfrak {ns}(t)$$\end{document}cns(t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\notin \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}t∉Q. To understand why, we next recall the methods of [28, Example 7.11].

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}ℓ∈C, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_\ell ,0)$$\end{document}L(cℓ,0) be the simple Virasoro vertex operator algebra at central charge\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s∈Z≥1 have lowest conformal weights\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} h_{r,s}(\ell ) =\frac{r^2-1}{4} \ell -\frac{rs-1}{2}+\frac{s^2-1}{4}\ell ^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}hr,s(ℓ)=r2-14ℓ-rs-12+s2-14ℓ-1.By [21, Theorem 4.2.6], the category of finite-length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_\ell ,0)$$\end{document}L(cℓ,0)-modules with composition factors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_\ell ,h_{r,s}(\ell ))$$\end{document}L(cℓ,hr,s(ℓ)) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s∈Z≥1 is a braided tensor category.

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ne 0,-1$$\end{document}t≠0,-1, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=\frac{1}{2}(t+1)$$\end{document}a=12(t+1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=\frac{1}{2}(t^{-1}+1)$$\end{document}b=12(t-1+1). The Virasoro vertex operator algebras \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a, 0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(cb,0) form a commuting pair of subalgebras in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c^\mathfrak {ns}(t), 0) \otimes \mathcal {F}(1)$$\end{document}S(cns(t),0)⊗F(1), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(1)$$\end{document}F(1) is the rank 1 free fermion vertex superalgebra of central charge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}12. See Appendix 4.4 for explicit expressions (involving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{t}$$\end{document}t) for the conformal vectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a,0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(cb,0) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c^{\mathfrak {ns}}(t),0)\otimes \mathcal {F}(1)$$\end{document}S(cns(t),0)⊗F(1). We can replace t with a formal variable x and view \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c^{\mathfrak {ns}}(x),0)$$\end{document}S(cns(x),0), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(1)$$\end{document}F(1), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a,0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(cb,0) as vertex operator (super)algebras over the field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}(x^{1/2})$$\end{document}C(x1/2); the explicit expressions in Appendix 4.4 show that we still have an embedding\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L(c_a,0)\otimes L(c_b,0)\hookrightarrow \mathcal {S}(c^{\mathfrak {ns}}(x),0)\otimes \mathcal {F}(1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(ca,0)⊗L(cb,0)↪S(cns(x),0)⊗F(1).Moreover, it follows from Corollary 2.6 and Theorem 2.10 of [19] (see also the proof of [19, Lemma 2.11]) that3.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}(c^\mathfrak {ns}(x), 0) \otimes \mathcal {F}(1)&\cong \bigoplus _{n=1}^\infty L(c_{a}, h_{1, n}(a))\otimes L(c_b, h_{1, n}(b)) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S(cns(x),0)⊗F(1)≅⨁n=1∞L(ca,h1,n(a))⊗L(cb,h1,n(b))as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a,0)\otimes L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0)⊗L(cb,0)-modules over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}(x^{1/2})$$\end{document}C(x1/2). This means that the decomposition still holds after specializing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\mapsto t$$\end{document}x↦t for all but countably many \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}t∈C. The methods of [28, Example 7.11] yield rigidity and fusion rules of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}^{\textrm{fin}}_{c^{\mathfrak {ns}}(t)}$$\end{document}Ocns(t)fin for all irrational t such that (3.16) holds, so we just need to prove this decomposition for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\notin \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}t∉Q:

Proposition 3.11

For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in \mathbb {C}\setminus \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}t∈C\Q,3.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}(c^\mathfrak {ns}(t), 0) \otimes \mathcal {F}(1) \cong \bigoplus _{n=1}^\infty L(c_{a}, h_{1, n}(a))\otimes L(c_b, h_{1, n}(b)) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S(cns(t),0)⊗F(1)≅⨁n=1∞L(ca,h1,n(a))⊗L(cb,h1,n(b))as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a,0)\otimes L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0)⊗L(cb,0)-modules, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=\frac{1}{2}(t+1)$$\end{document}a=12(t+1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=\frac{1}{2}(t^{-1}+1)$$\end{document}b=12(t-1+1).

Proof

The decomposition (3.16) implies that for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}n∈Z≥1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c^{\mathfrak {ns}}(x),0)\otimes \mathcal {F}(1)$$\end{document}S(cns(x),0)⊗F(1) contains a non-zero vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_n$$\end{document}vn of conformal weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{1,n}(a)+h_{1,n}(b)=\frac{(n-1)^2}{2}$$\end{document}h1,n(a)+h1,n(b)=(n-1)22 which is highest weight for both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a,0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(cb,0). To complete the proof, it now suffices to show that both sides of (3.17) have the same character. The basis (3.18) implies that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{ch}[\mathcal {S}(c^{\mathfrak {ns}}(t),0)\otimes \mathcal {F}(1)] = q^{-(8-3t-3t^{-1})/24}\prod Verma modules for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{ch}\left[ \bigoplus _{n=1}^\infty L(c_{a}, h_{1, n}(a))\otimes L(c_b, h_{1, n}(b))\right] = q^{-(8-3t-3t^{-1})/24}\sum _{n=1}^\infty \frac{q^{(n-1)^2/2}(1-q^n)^2}{\prod _{m=1}^\infty (1-q^m)^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ch⨁n=1∞L(ca,h1,n(a))⊗L(cb,h1,n(b))=q-(8-3t-3t-1)/24∑n=1∞q(n-1)2/2(1-qn)2∏m=1∞(1-qm)2.Multiplying both expressions by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} prove3.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \prod _{m=1}^\infty (1-q^m)(1+q^{m-1/2})^2 =\sum _{n=1}^\infty \frac{q^{(n-1)^2/2}(1-q^n)^2}{1-q^{1/2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∏m=1∞(1-qm)(1+qm-1/2)2=∑n=1∞q(n-1)2/2(1-qn)21-q1/2.The right side is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{n=0}^\infty \frac{q^{n^2/2}(1-q^{n+1})^2}{1-q^{1/2}}&=\sum _{n=0}^\infty \frac{(q^{n^2/2}-q^{(n+1)^2/2+1/2})(1-q^{n+1})}{1-q^{1/2}}\nonumber \\&= \sum _{n=0}^\infty \frac{q^{n^2/2}(1-q^{n+1})}{1-q^{1/2}} -\sum _{n=1}^\infty \frac{q^{n^2/2} q^{1/2}(1-q^n)}{1-q^{1/2}}\nonumber \\&= \frac{1-q}{1-q^{1/2}} +\sum _{n=1}^\infty \frac{q^{n^2/2}(1-q^{1/2}+q^{n+1/2}-q^{n+1})}{1-q^{1/2}}\nonumber \\&= 1+q^{1/2}+\sum _{n=1}^\infty q^{n^2/2}(1+q^{n+1/2})\nonumber \\&= \sum _{n=0}^\infty (q^{n^2/2}+q^{(n+1)^2/2}) =\sum _{n=-\infty }^\infty q^{n^2/2}. (3.19) follows from the Jacobi triple product identity, completing the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Now that we have the decomposition (3.16) for all irrational t, the same arguments as in [28, Example 7.11] prove rigidity and determine fusion rules for the category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^{fin }$$\end{document}Ocfin. The category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_{c}^\textrm{fin}$$\end{document}Ocfin is rigid and slightly degenerate, that is, its Müger center is semisimple with simple objects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_{1,1}$$\end{document}S1,1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi (\mathcal {S}_{1,1})$$\end{document}Π(S1,1).

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s, r',s' \in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s,r′,s′∈Z≥1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r+s, r'+s' \in 2\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}r+s,r′+s′∈2Z, 3.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_{r,s}\boxtimes \mathcal {S}_{r',s'} \cong \bigoplus _{\begin{array}{c} r'' = |r-r'|+1\\ r+r'+r''\, \textrm{odd} \end{array}}^{r+r'-1}\bigoplus _{\begin{array}{c} s'' = |s-s'|+1\\ s+s'+s''\, \textrm{odd} \end{array}}^{s+s'-1} \Pi ^{(r+r'-r''+s+s'-s''-2)/2}(\mathcal {S}_{r'',s''}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sr,s⊠Sr′,s′≅⨁r′′=|r-r′|+1r+r′+r′′oddr+r′-1⨁s′′=|s-s′|+1s+s′+s′′odds+s′-1Π(r+r′-r′′+s+s′-s′′-2)/2(Sr′′,s′′).

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s, r',s' \in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s,r′,s′∈Z≥1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r+s, r'+s' \in 2\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}r+s,r′+s′∈2Z, 3.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_{r,s}\boxtimes \mathcal {S}_{r',s'} \cong \bigoplus _{\begin{array}{c} r'' = |r-r'|+1\\ r+r'+r''\, \textrm{odd} \end{array}}^{r+r'-1}\bigoplus _{\begin{array}{c} s'' = |s-s'|+1\\ s+s'+s''\, \textrm{odd} \end{array}}^{s+s'-1} \Pi ^{(r+r'-r''+s+s'-s''-2)/2}(\mathcal {S}_{r'',s''}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sr,s⊠Sr′,s′≅⨁r′′=|r-r′|+1r+r′+r′′oddr+r′-1⨁s′′=|s-s′|+1s+s′+s′′odds+s′-1Π(r+r′-r′′+s+s′-s′′-2)/2(Sr′′,s′′).

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s∈Z≥1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r+s\in 2\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}r+s∈2Z, 3.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_{r,s} \otimes \mathcal {F}(1) \cong \bigoplus _{n=1}^\infty L(c_{a}, h_{r, n}(a))\otimes L(c_b, h_{s, n}(b)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sr,s⊗F(1)≅⨁n=1∞L(ca,hr,n(a))⊗L(cb,hs,n(b)). as an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a,0)\otimes L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0)⊗L(cb,0)-module.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s∈Z≥1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r+s\in 2\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}r+s∈2Z, 3.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_{r,s} \otimes \mathcal {F}(1) \cong \bigoplus _{n=1}^\infty L(c_{a}, h_{r, n}(a))\otimes L(c_b, h_{s, n}(b)). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sr,s⊗F(1)≅⨁n=1∞L(ca,hr,n(a))⊗L(cb,hs,n(b)). as an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a,0)\otimes L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0)⊗L(cb,0)-module.

Proof

(1) follows from (3.22) and (3.23), and then (1) implies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}^{\textrm{fin}}_c$$\end{document}Ocfin is rigid since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {C}_{c_a}^{Vir} \boxtimes \mathcal {C}_{c_{b}}^{Vir})_0$$\end{document}(CcaVir⊠CcbVir)0 is rigid [21, Section 5] and parity reversals of rigid modules are rigid. For (3), the monoidal embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {C}_{c_a}^{Vir} \boxtimes \mathcal {C}_{c_{b}}^{Vir})_0\hookrightarrow \mathcal {O}_c^{\textrm{fin}}$$\end{document}(CcaVir⊠CcbVir)0↪Ocfin and the fusion rules from [21] imply\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Pi ^{(r+s-2)/2}(\mathcal {S}_{r,s})\boxtimes \Pi ^{(r'+s'-2)/2}(\mathcal {S}_{r',s'}) \cong \bigoplus _{\begin{array}{c} r'' = |r-r'|+1\\ r+r'+r''\, \textrm{odd} \end{array}}^{r+r'-1}\bigoplus _{\begin{array}{c} s'' = |s-s'|+1\\ s+s'+s''\, \textrm{odd} \end{array}}^{s+s'-1} \Pi ^{(r''+s''-2)/2}(\mathcal {S}_{r'',s''}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Π(r+s-2)/2(Sr,s)⊠Π(r′+s′-2)/2(Sr′,s′)≅⨁r′′=|r-r′|+1r+r′+r′′oddr+r′-1⨁s′′=|s-s′|+1s+s′+s′′odds+s′-1Π(r′′+s′′-2)/2(Sr′′,s′′).Then Proposition 2.19 yields (3.24).

For the slight degeneracy statement in (2), suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{r,s})$$\end{document}Πn(Sr,s) is in the Müger center of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}^{\textrm{fin}}_c$$\end{document}Ocfin. Then in particular the double braiding\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {R}^2: \Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{r,s})\boxtimes \Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{2,2}) \xrightarrow {\sim } \Pi \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R2:Πn(Sr,s)⊠Πn(S2,2)→∼Πn(S2,2)⊠Πn(Sr,s)→∼Πn(Sr,s)⊠Πn(S2,2)is the identity, and by (3.24) and Proposition 2.19,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} _{\delta ,\varepsilon \in \lbrace \pm 1\rbrace } \Pi ^{(1-\delta \varepsilon )/2}(\mathcal {S}_{r+\delta ,s+\varepsilon }), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Πn(Sr,s)⊠Πn(S2,2)≅Sr+1,s+1⊕Π(Sr+1,s-1)⊕Π(Sr-1,s+1)⊕Sr-1,s-1=⨁δ,ε∈{±1}Π(1-δε)/2(Sr+δ,s+ε),with the convention \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal By (2.19) and the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0$$\end{document}L0-conjugation property for intertwining operators,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathcal {R}^2(\left( \mathcal {Y}_{\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{r,s}),\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{2,2})}(w_{r,s},x)w_{2,2}\right) = \mathcal {Y}_{\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{r,s}),\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{2,2})}(w_{r,s},e^{2\pi i}x)w_{2,2}\nonumber \\&\qquad = e^{2\pi i(L_0-\textrm{wt}\,w_{r,s}-\textrm{wt}\,w_{2,2})}\mathcal {Y}_{\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{r,s}),\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{2,2})}(w_{r,s},x)w_{2,2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R2(YΠn(Sr,s),Πn(S2,2)(wr,s,x)w2,2=YΠn(Sr,s),Πn(S2,2)(wr,s,e2πix)w2,2=e2πi(L0-wtwr,s-wtw2,2)YΠn(Sr,s),Πn(S2,2)(wr,s,x)w2,2for homogeneous \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{r,s}\in \Pi (\mathcal {S}_{r,s}), w_{2,2}\in \Pi (\mathcal {S}_{2,2})$$\end{document}wr,s∈Π(Sr,s),w2,2∈Π(S2,2). Conversely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{1,1})$$\end{document}Πn(S1,1) is in the Müger center for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=0,1$$\end{document}n=0,1 because the double braiding on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi ^m(\mathcal {S}_{r,s})\boxtimes \Pi ^n(\mathcal {S}_{1,1})\cong \Pi ^{m+n}(\mathcal {S}_{r,s})$$\end{document}Πm(Sr,s)⊠Πn(S1,1)≅Πm+n(Sr,s) is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e^{2\pi i(h_{r,s}^{\mathfrak {ns}}+(m+n)/2-h^{\mathfrak {ns}}_{r,s}-m/2-h^\mathfrak {ns}_{1,1}-n/2)} =1 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e2πi(hr,sns+(m+n)/2-hr,sns-m/2-h1,1ns-n/2)=1for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}r,s∈Z≥1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m=0,1$$\end{document}m=0,1.

Finally, (4) follows from (3.20) and (3.21) since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_{r,s}\otimes \mathcal {F}(1)$$\end{document}Sr,s⊗F(1) has the same \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(c_a,0)\otimes L(c_b,0)$$\end{document}L(ca,0)⊗L(cb,0)-module decomposition as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi (\mathcal {S}_{r,s})\otimes \mathcal {F}(1)=\Pi (\mathcal {S}_{r,s}\otimes \mathcal {F}(1))$$\end{document}Π(Sr,s)⊗F(1)=Π(Sr,s⊗F(1)). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Specifically, we show in Appendix 4.4 that we can take3.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_{2,1}^a\otimes u_{2,1}^b=\frac{2e^{\pi i/4}\sqrt{t}}{t-1} G_{-\frac{1}{2}}v_{2,2}\otimes \textbf{1}_{\mathcal {F}(1)} +e^{-\pi i/4}\, v_{2,2}\otimes \psi _{-\frac{1}{2}}\textbf{1}_{\mathcal {F}(1)} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u2,1a⊗u2,1b=2eπi/4tt-1G-12v2,2⊗1F(1)+e-πi/4v2,2⊗ψ-121F(1).On the other hand, we identify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{2,2}^a\otimes u_{2,2}^b = v_{2,2}\otimes \textbf{1}_{\mathcal {F}(1)}$$\end{document}u2,2a⊗u2,2b=v2,2⊗1F(1) since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{2,2}(a)+h_{2,2}(b)=h^{\mathfrak {ns}}_{2,2}(t)$$\end{document}h2,2(a)+h2,2(b)=h2,2ns(t).

Tensor structure at central charge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{3}{2}$$\end{document}32

Now we consider the module category of the “degenerate minimal model” vertex operator superalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(c^\mathfrak {ns}(1),0)=\mathcal {S}(\frac{3}{2},0)$$\end{document}S(cns(1),0)=S(32,0) studied by Milas in [74]. For central charge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{3}{2}$$\end{document}32, reducible Verma modules have lowest conformal weights\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H_{3/2}&=\lbrace h_{r,s}^\mathfrak {ns}(1)\mid r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1},\,r-s\in 2\mathbb {Z}\rbrace \\&=\left\{ \frac{(r-s)^2}{8}\mid r,s\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 1},\,r-s\in 2\mathbb {Z}\right\} =\lbrace h_{2n+1,1}^\mathfrak {ns}(1)\mid n\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}\rbrace . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H3/2={hr,sns(1)∣r,s∈Z≥1,r-s∈2Z}=(r-s)28∣r,s∈Z≥1,r-s∈2Z={h2n+1,1ns(1)∣n∈Z≥0}.Thus the simple modules in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {O}_{3/2}^{\textrm{fin}}$$\end{document}O3/2fin are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lbrace \mathcal {S}_{2n+1,1}\mid n\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}\rbrace $$\end{document}{S2n+1,1∣n∈Z≥0}. The embedding diagrams for the corresponding Verma modules are given in Proposition 2.5(1)(d), and they show that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}_{2n+1,1}\cong M^\mathfrak {ns}_{2n+1,1}/M^\mathfrak {ns}_{2n+3,1} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S2n+1,1≅M2n+1,1ns/M2n+3,1nsfor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}n∈Z≥0. Although \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}^{\textrm{fin}}_{3/2}$$\end{document}O3/2fin is not semisimple, we will show that it has a semisimple tensor subcategory that contains all simple objects.

To do so, we use the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(\frac{3}{2}, 0)$$\end{document}S(32,0) is the SO(3)-fixed point subalgebra of the vertex operator superalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(3)$$\end{document}F(3) of three free fermions. Note that the full automorphism group of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(3)$$\end{document}F(3) is O(3), generated by SO(3) together with the parity automorphism. The decomposition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(3)$$\end{document}F(3) as an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SO(3) \otimes \mathcal {S}(\frac{3}{2}, 0)$$\end{document}SO(3)⊗S(32,0)-module is easily computed:

Lemma 3.13

As an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SO(3) \otimes \mathcal {S}(\frac{3}{2}, 0)$$\end{document}SO(3)⊗S(32,0)-module,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}(3) \cong \bigoplus _{n=0 }^\infty \rho _{2n+1} \otimes \Pi ^n\left( \mathcal {S}_{2n+1,1}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(3)≅⨁n=0∞ρ2n+1⊗ΠnS2n+1,1,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{2n+1}$$\end{document}ρ2n+1 denotes the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2n+1)$$\end{document}(2n+1)-dimensional simple SO(3)-module.

Proof

As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(3)$$\end{document}F(3) is a continuous SO(3)-module with finite-dimensional conformal weight spaces,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}(3) \cong \bigoplus _{n=0 }^\infty \rho _{2n+1} \otimes L_{2n} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(3)≅⨁n=0∞ρ2n+1⊗L2nas an SO(3)-module, where by [60, Theorem 1.1], the multiplicity spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{2n}$$\end{document}L2n are simple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}(\frac{3}{2}, 0)$$\end{document}S(32,0)-modules. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m\in \frac{1}{2}\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}m∈12Z and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}n∈Z, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{m, n}$$\end{document}Fm,n be the subspace of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(3)$$\end{document}F(3) of conformal weight m and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {so}(3)$$\end{document}so(3)-weight n. Since the three fermion strong generators of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(3)$$\end{document}F(3) have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm 2$$\end{document}±2 and 0, the graded dimension of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(3)$$\end{document}F(3) has the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{ch}[\mathcal {F}(3)](z, q)= \sum _{m\in \frac{1}{2}\mathbb {Z},\,n \in 2\mathbb {Z}} \dim (F_{m, n}) z^n q^m \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ch[F(3)](z,q)=∑m∈12Z,n∈2Zdim(Fm,n)znqmLet\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_{2n}(q) = \sum \limits _{ m \in \frac{1}{2} \mathbb {Z}} \text {dim}(F_{m,2n}) q^m \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F2n(q)=∑m∈12Zdim(Fm,2n)qmbe the coefficient of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z^{2n}$$\end{document}z2n, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {ch}[L_{2n}]= F_{2n}(q) - F_{2n+2}(q)$$\end{document}ch[L2n]=F2n(q)-F2n+2(q).

Now, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {ch}[\mathcal {F}(3)](z, q)$$\end{document}ch[F(3)](z,q) has the nice product form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{n \in \mathbb {Z}} z^{2n} q^{n^2/2} = \prod _{m=1}^\infty (1 + z^2 q^{m-1/2} )(1 - q^{m} )(1 + z^{-2} q^{m-1/2} ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑n∈Zz2nqn2/2=∏m=1∞(1+z2qm-1/2)(1-qm)(1+z-2qm-1/2),this simplifies to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym}
